- **History**, from every browser, Arc's too: the pages of the last 90 days, as far back as Chromium keeps it, with their titles, visit and typed counts (Firefox keeps only whether a page was typed, Safari not even that), into the profile its Spaces go to, marked imported. A browser's database is read from a copy with its write-ahead log, as the browser keeps it locked while it runs. A page already there with a visit as late is skipped, so a rerun adds nothing, and the summary counts only the pages that were new.
- **Favorites from history**, ours (#1390): an import's end in Settings offers the favorites it worked out from the history, as onboarding's Your favorites does, under what came: the same line, then the same tiles in a group of their own, and Add 7 Favorites, counting those ticked, before Done, "Add Favorites" dimmed while none is ticked (#1673). It adds those ticked to the first Space of the profile the browser started with and says how many after what came, and the offer goes. Onboarding's step takes the offer, whether it adds or goes on with none, so the import's end doesn't make it again; skipped, the import's end still offers it. A passwords file's end offers none.

### Onboarding

A new profile's first launch (*Ours*: Arc's and Dia's are films and sign-ups of theirs, so only their length, pacing and quiet are taken, never their images, sounds or names). It plays only when the launch has nothing to bring back and nothing to open; a profile with Spaces, so anyone updating, never sees it, and a normal launch pays nothing for it, since that launch never reaches its code or its face. Agents have no path through it.

- **Where.** Over the whole window, drawn by the UI, while it shows no page: the brand's ground, Paper (#F3F0E8) with Ink (#16140F) on it in a light appearance and the other way round in a dark one, as the app icon's tile and stem, and the Signal green (#0E9F55) only on the wordmark's dot and a step's one button. The window's buttons stay.
- **The intro.** The wordmark (a dot, two stems and a ring, ilo-studio's `brand/logo/wordmark.svg` at a half, 107 × 77) draws itself in the window's middle, the dot takes the green, then "a good tool." comes under it in the display face (Motion › Intro). No sound. Any key or click skips it to the first step.
- **Steps.** One message a screen: a title in the display face (32 pt), one line under it (15 pt at 0.6 of the ink), and one button in the Signal green with Paper words and its key's chip, ↩. Skip sits quietly at the window's bottom right, 24 in, with Esc's chip. Esc or Skip goes on to the next step without doing the step's thing, ↩ does it, and after the last the flow fades from over the first Space (Motion › Onboarding step, Onboarding end).
  - Bring your stuff: the import view Settings › Import & Export shows (Import from other browsers), 556 wide on the flow's ground, its boxes the ink at 0.05 over it and its words in the ink. The step's title, line, button and corner follow the state the view shows (#1673): the browsers, "Bring your stuff." and "Spaces, tabs and history from the browser you used. It all stays on this Mac." ("this computer" off the Mac), the one used last picked on a plate with ↩ at its end, which ↑ ↓ and a click move, and under them one quiet line, "Another browser or computer? Bring a bookmarks file or a passwords file.", each a link to the open panel; the button Import from Arc and the corner Skip. A browser's choices, "What comes from Arc." and "All of it, unless you turn something off. Tabs load when you first open them.", the button Import and the corner Back. Safari's wait, "Let ilo read Safari." and why, the button Open System Settings and the corner Back. An import that can still stop, "Here it comes." and "From Arc. It goes on by itself once your Spaces are in.", with the view's Cancel and no corner. The button is the flow's own, with its ↩ chip, and ↩ presses it; Esc goes back to the list from the choices and the wait, cancels an import that can still stop, and skips the step from the list. The view then has none of its own buttons for those states. Left out when it finds no browser to import from. The step stays until the import has read what it brings, as the view may still need the user: a browser whose files need Full Disk Access shows the view's way to System Settings, with Skip, and a passwords file's end offers Delete File, its Done going on (#1378, #1379). The step stays while the Spaces come, "Here it comes.", holds 0.6 s once they're in, and then goes on by itself while the rest runs, its line ("Importing from Arc…"), then what came in, quietly at the window's bottom left, 24 in, through the steps after it (#1673). A profile that hasn't opened keeps it, "Work hasn't opened.", with Try Again as its button and Skip in its corner; a stopped import says "Stopped." with Import Again and Skip. The window keeps the view once it's made, so an import under way goes on once the flow ends, and Welcome to ilo shows the same one again.
  - Your favorites (owner, 2026-10-29): only once an import has read the history. The sites the imported history visited most, worked out on the machine and sent nowhere: eight at most, a site once however its pages and `www.` differ, at its root, visited three times or more, and not the user's own machine or network. Each is a tile, drawn by the import's view for both hosts (#1673): 76 tall at the row radius, four to a row across the view's 556, 8 apart, the site's letter on a 22 plate over its name, 11/14, and the cards' badge at its top right, the green with a tick, which a click unticks to an empty ring, the tile then an outline with its letter and name at 0.4. The badge changes as Motion › Glyph change does; the tiles land as the import's cards come, 50 ms apart in order of most visits, fading in place under Reduce Motion. Under them, "They go at the top of your first Space, as tiles like these." ↩ (Add 7 Favorites, counting those ticked, or Add 1 Favorite) makes those ticked the first Space's favorites; with none ticked the button is Continue. Left out when it offers none, and when the import brought favorites of its own, as Arc's does.
  - Make ilo your default browser: its line says what the system does and where its way back is, in General's row's words for each system, and Make Default asks the system as that row does. In a Flatpak, which can't set it, the line says where the desktop does and the button is Continue. Left out while ilo is the default already.
  - Pick a colour for your first Space: the theme picker's nine swatches in a row, 28 across, 40 apart, none picked to begin with; ← and → go along them and a click picks one, ringed in the ink. ↩ gives the Space the one picked, as the picker's swatch does, one step for ⌘Z; Skip leaves the Space as it was.
  - Your Spaces on every computer: Settings › Sync's own view (Settings › Sync), drawn on the flow's ground as the import's is, 556 wide. Its line says what syncs, that it's encrypted end to end and that Settings has it too, so the view's box says nothing more while signed out: Sign In is the step's button, with "Server: ilo · Change…" centred under it. The sign-in window, the account's first device's recovery words and the code another device shows behave as in Settings, and the flow goes on by itself once the account syncs, after Done on the words. The words show once, so while they do there's no Skip and Esc does nothing. Left out in a build that names no sync server, and once the browser is signed in, which the core may say only after the flow has started. Agents get no path to it, as with Settings › Sync.
  - Up to you: Settings' own switches, both off, Send crash reports (#452) and Let agents use the browser (#450), in one box as Settings' Privacy has them: 420 wide, the ink at 0.05 over the ground as the import's boxes are, with Settings' padding and hairline between the rows. Each row is its switch's name, Settings' row's, in medium over one sentence at body (the agents' is their row's while it's off), wrapping inside the box, and the switch in the green at the box's right edge, centred on its row, so the switches line up. A click anywhere on a row, the switch included, turns it, and it's set as it's turned, as in Settings, so Skip goes on with it as it was left. Crash reports show only once there's a collector to send them to, as Settings' Privacy has it, and the agents' always, as every build has their server.
- **In.** The first Space, with the first run's welcome on its empty card and the command bar up over it, as ⌘T has it, once the flow has faded. One line sits at the card's foot, 40 up, at body in the card's secondary words: "Type a site, a search or a command.", ⌘T's chip, "brings it back from anywhere." It goes when a page shows. A page that ends the flow brings no bar.
- **Seeing it again.** Help › Welcome to ilo, and its row in the command bar, play it again: in the window when it shows no page, else in a new window as ⌘N's, since a page covers the UI. A page shown ends it.
- **Checks.** Every check run starts a new profile, so check.sh skips it with `BROWSER_ONBOARDING=skip`, and the onboarding run sets `light` or `dark` instead, for its captures, with the import reading the made-up Arc the import run does.

### Updates

Each system's own updater downloads an update in the background and then says it's ready, and the user restarts into it when they choose, the same on every OS (owner, 2026-11-02, #1416, #1418). The Mac's and Windows' read one appcast, signed with one EdDSA key, whose URL is a default service URL, so forks change it (principle 2).

- **Mac.** Sparkle 2 (Dia and Ardia, P§24). It checks every 8 hours (**Ardia**), downloads a newer build in the background, and installs it as ilo quits, after macOS's check of the new build, which it starts warm.
- **Windows.** ilo's own updater, for a copy from winget or the website (#1418): every 8 hours, and when asked, it reads the appcast over https for the newest Windows installer whose build is above its own, downloads it in the background, checks its EdDSA signature against the key built in and holds it unchanged until it runs. Restart to Update quits ilo and runs it silently, and it starts ilo again, with its windows and tabs, once it's in; any other quit runs it without the relaunch, so the next launch is new, as on the Mac. An installer that fails, or never starts, leaves the next launch the old build, which says "The update didn’t install" with Try Again and checks again only when asked or 8 hours on (#1459). Nothing shows a window of its own, and an installer that isn't signed, isn't newer or isn't https never runs. A copy from the Microsoft Store is the Store's to update.
- **Linux.** ilo is the Flatpak, and Flatpak's portal is the updater: it looks every half hour, and ilo installs a build it announces beside the running one at once, then says it's ready as the others do. Outside a Flatpak, as a dev build, nothing updates ilo.
- **The update banner** (Arc's, the owner's call, #678). Once an update is downloaded and waiting, or on the Mac once Sparkle has found one it won't download by itself, such as one that needs the user's go-ahead, a pill at the sidebar's foot says so: 24 tall, 6 over the footer, at the row plate's inset and radius, in the pressed plate's ink, with the circle-fading-arrow-up glyph in the rows' icon column and "Update ready" or "Update available" on their title column in small type at "+ New Tab"'s ink, the version at its end. The list gives up its room, and it sits over the footer and under any media player. Under the pointer it opens upward to 86, over the players and the list's last rows, on the window's opaque look under a hovered plate, in the dialog's timing from its first frame, and not at all under Reduce Motion: "ilo 0.1.5 is ready" with "Your tabs come back." and Restart to Update, or "ilo 0.1.5 is available" with "See what’s new before it installs." and Show Update, which opens Sparkle's window on it. It closes as the pointer leaves. A click on a ready update says Restarting… and "ilo reopens with your tabs.", open, until the quit goes ahead; the update installs only then, and a page that asks to stay stops it, puts the banner back open and leaves nothing armed, the next plain quit installing without the relaunch (#1459). It's in every window, private ones too, has no toast, no badge and no modal, and the restart that installs the update takes it away. While Check for updates automatically is off, which makes every update a manual one, the open banner is 22 taller, with Update Automatically under its button in small secondary words that brighten under the pointer: a click turns the checks back on, as About's switch does, and the offer goes, as Arc's banner offers after a manual update (#678). Turned on or off in Settings while an update waits, the banner follows at once.
- **Settings › About.** Its first row, the app's name over its version, says at its end where the update stands, in the row's secondary words, with a button where one moves it on: Check for Updates at rest; "Checking…"; "ilo is up to date" with Check for Updates; "ilo 0.1.1 is available", and on the Mac, for one Sparkle won't download by itself, with Show Update, which opens Sparkle's window on it; "Downloading 0.1.1…"; "ilo 0.1.1 is ready" with Restart to Update; "Couldn’t check for updates" or "The update didn’t install" with Try Again. The button runs the same check as Check for Updates… without a window of its own, and the row follows it. A copy from the Microsoft Store says "The Microsoft Store updates ilo", and a build with no updater says nothing, both without a button.
- **Nightly builds.** Under the version row, in a build whose updater can follow it, the Mac's and the Windows installer's: "Get nightly builds", with a switch and "Each night’s build of ilo, ahead of the next release." under it (#871). On, each check reads the nightly feed, and turning it on checks at once; a nightly build says "Nightly" where a release says "Version", and starts on. Off on a nightly, the words become "ilo keeps this build until a release is newer.": no updater goes back a build, so the nightly stays until a release passes it. The choice is the app's, not a profile's, and outlives a relaunch. The nightly feed is a default service URL beside the release's (`branding/update-feed-nightly`), so a build without one, the Store's copy and a Flatpak, which follows the branch it's installed from, have no row. Agents can't change it.
- **Check for Updates….** In the app menu, and in the command bar, as Linux shows no menu bar. It's Restart to Update while an update waits. On the Mac it's Sparkle's own window, as before. On Windows it opens Settings › About, whose row says where the update stands. On Linux it says where the update stands, or tries a failed install again. A build with no updater doesn't offer it.
- **Agents.** The core has no update state, so agents can't see one; none is added.
- ilo needs macOS 15 (Sequoia) or later, the owner's call (#678); Arc needs 13 (Ventura). The bundle says so (`LSMinimumSystemVersion`), so macOS won't open it on an older Mac and the feed offers that Mac no update. README.md and each release's notes name it.
- An install never starts by itself while ilo runs: it waits for Restart to Update or a quit, so a window in full screen is never interrupted. A scheduled check shows no alert of Sparkle's own, in full screen or not: what it finds is the banner's. Check for Updates… shows Sparkle's window, as the user asked.
- The first launch after an update opens its release notes, its entry in the site's changelog, as a tab in the main window, as What's New does. The Mac's and Windows' updaters keep the notes as they install the update, for its first launch. On Linux, where the update can come from outside ilo (GNOME Software, `flatpak update`), the profile keeps the last version that ran, and a newer one opens its notes. A first run or a downgrade opens nothing (#1495).
- Measure Arc's update prompt, if one can still be triggered.

### Safe start

Ours (the owner's call, #2345): Arc and Chrome have none. When ilo crashes at launch twice in a row, the next launch starts with extensions and Boosts not loaded, so an extension or a Boost that crashes it can't keep it from opening.

- **At launch** is the first minute after the window shows. A crash after it counts nothing, as one hours into use isn't a launch problem. The count is one small mark in the app's folder, "Last launch", saying where the last launch got to. It's written as the window shows, once more a minute later by a single timer, with nothing after it, and as ilo quits. It's never sent anywhere.
- **What's held.** The profile's extensions, the ad blocker included, are held for that launch before any of them runs. That's Chrome's own state for an extension whose process crashed, so nothing is saved. Chrome's extensions page still lists each one with its switch as the user left it, and its Remove. The browser's own component extensions, themes, and any extension a policy keeps on stay. Boosts don't show, and Settings' Boosts switch reads as the user left it.
- **The card.** It sits at the sidebar's foot, 6 over the footer, under the update banner and any media player. It has the row plate's inset and radius, and the pressed plate's ink on the window's opaque look.
  - The circle-alert glyph sits in the rows' icon column, on the first line of the title.
  - The title, "ilo closed unexpectedly twice", is in body type, medium weight.
  - Under it, in small secondary words: "Extensions and Boosts are off for now, so ads aren’t blocked.", or without the last clause while Settings' ad blocking is off.
  - Both wrap on the title column, and the card grows to their lines, as the list gives up its room.
  - Below them come Turn Back On, an accent button, and "Manage Extensions…" in small secondary words that brighten under the pointer. It opens Chrome's extensions page in a new tab.
  - It has no ×: the hold lasts the launch, so the card does too. It's in every window, private ones too, with no toast, badge or modal.
- **The way out.** Turn Back On restarts ilo with its windows and tabs into a normal launch. Any quit ends a safe start too, so the next launch is normal.
- **Agents** can't start or end one: no tool does either. The MCP server stays up through it.
- **Every OS** is the same: the mark, the count and the card are the app's, and the core holds the extensions on each.

### Sync

Ours, through Settings › Sync (Settings). The account keeps the same on each of its devices: the Spaces, with their names, colours, icons, themes, pinned tabs, folders and Today tabs in their order, and the first profile's bookmarks, passwords and open tabs. An agent's Space stays on its device, and so do a window's own Space, a private window and Little ilo. The other computers' tabs outside their Spaces are found in the command bar (Command bar › Rows). The server holds it all encrypted with the account's secret, which only the devices have, and knows a device only by its kind, such as "ilo on Mac", never by its name.

- **A new device's first Space.** A browser makes a Space on its first run. When sync first brings the account's Spaces to a device, that Space goes while it's the device's only one and the user hasn't made it theirs: no name or icon of theirs, no pinned tabs or folders, no favorites. A colour or theme, as onboarding's first step gives it, is setup and doesn't keep it. Its Today tabs move to the top of the account's first Space, in their order, so nothing open goes. One the user made theirs stays beside the account's and syncs as any other. That's Arc's way on a new device (the owner). The account's first device keeps its own, as the account has none yet.
- **A newer ilo.** When a newer version on another device writes the Spaces in a form this one can't read whole, a new kind or field, this device stops syncing them, rather than write over what it can't read, and Settings › Sync says "Update ilo to keep syncing Spaces." The rest still syncs, and once it's updated the account sends the Spaces again. Arc paused its sync the same way, for a device on an old Arc; ilo says it on the device that has to update, which needs no other device's version.
- **Today tabs** (ours, the owner's call, 2026-11-08, #2098: Arc kept them on the device). Each Space's Today tabs are the same on every device, near real time, about 2 s from a change to the other sidebar. Opening, closing or moving one, or its page going to a new address, sends it, a burst of them as one change; a title goes with the next. A tab from another device comes as a row without its page, which loads only when it's clicked, so sync costs the device no page loads. A tab shown here keeps its page when another device navigates it. Closed on one device, it closes on the others into their archives, as if closed there. Auto-archive counts from the tab's last use on any device, which a device sends at most about once an hour a tab. A page that can't open on another device, or that holds the page itself, such as a data: URL, isn't sent: the account keeps the tab's last page that was. The selected tab, scroll, back and forward, page state and the archive stay on the device.
- **Too soon.** A device that joins before the first device's Spaces reach the server, in the seconds after the first device turns sync on, finds none, so it keeps its own first Space and the account has both (*interim*).
- **Also sync** (ours, #1235). Under the account, two switches choose what else this device syncs, each device for itself, as Chrome's Customize sync does: Settings, and Extensions. A device that signs in starts with both on; one that synced before keeps its own until the user turns them on. They show only while sync is on, and agents have no path to them.
- **Settings that follow the account**: Little ilo on or off, and when its windows go to the archive; Warn before quitting, Restore windows and Warn before moving tabs to another profile; Ad blocking, Relay calls, Peek and Picture in picture; which hover previews show; Ask where to save each file; whether sites can ask to send notifications; View › Appearance, as Chrome's Light, Dark and Device follow its account, so Automatic has each computer follow its own system; and Show Space Header. Each is the account's alone, so two devices changing two of them at once both keep theirs, and the server sees neither name nor value. Turning Settings on takes the account's, as the device joins what's there; turning it off keeps what came. What stays with the computer: everything a profile holds (its search engine, download folder, archive time, passwords offer, notes), as only the first profile syncs; what's this machine's hardware, screen or memory (haptics, ⌥ revealing the sidebar, page zoom, unloading idle tabs, Battery Saver); keyboard shortcuts; consents given on this machine (crash reports, Widevine); the default browser, link rules, Agents' CLIs, nightly builds and the menu bar's button. Chromium's own preferences and search engines don't sync, as ilo's settings are ilo's.
- **Extensions** from the Chrome Web Store install by themselves on the account's other devices, with Chrome's EXTENSIONS and EXTENSION_SETTINGS, and removing one removes it there. Each installs from the store, as one the user adds does.

## Phase 4 features

### Peek

Ardia's docs record nothing of Arc's Peek. How it opens, closes and expands into a tab is in the capture plan. Its look is ours (the owner, 2026-11-02, #1373).

- **Which clicks open it.** A link to another site clicked in a pinned tab or a favorite (Arc). And any web link the user asks to see in it, in any tab of theirs, Today tabs too (the owner, 2026-11-02, #1370): ⇧-click and ⌥⇧-click, which Chrome opens in a new window, and Open Link in Peek in the link's menu, after Open Link in New Tab. An asked-for link always opens in Peek, growing from where the user pressed or asked for the menu: link rules and meeting links route only the ones a plain click opens. With Peek off in Settings, ⇧-click opens a foreground tab (Mouse) and the menu has no Open Link in Peek. Agents' tabs get none, as Peek is the user's.

It floats over the page, so it is a child window (decision 4). While it's open, ⇧⌘C copies its page's address, not the tab's under it, with ⇧⌘C's toast (Arc's Copy Link copies the Peek's URL).

- **Look.** The page is the card: edge to edge with corners of 16, inside the modal level's 1 pt rim, with that level's outline and shadow, as the dialog floats, and no frame of its own. Its controls float over the dim, 8 above the card's top edge and level with its sides, on round plates 32 tall in the palette's overlay at the popover level. The site is at the left: its favicon, then its host in body type at medium, without www., a port or a user. Open as Tab (⌘↩) and Close are at the right, 8 apart, with Little ilo's bar's glyphs. Each names itself and its key in the app's tooltip, as the header's buttons do: Open as Tab ⌘↩ and Close ⎋ (the owner, 2026-11-02, #1410). The whole window under it, sidebar too, takes the dialog's dim at 0.4, lighter than the dialog's, so the page under it reads as the context the link came from. The card sits in from the window's page by 10% of the page's smaller side, from 24 to 120, with 40 more at the top for the controls. As it grows into the tab's place on ⌘↩, its corners turn to the window's page's, so it lands without a step. On Linux and Windows the page shows through a hole in the preview's window, so nothing there casts a shadow, which would fall on the page: the rim draws round the hole. Its motion, opening, closing and growing into a tab, is unchanged (the owner, 2026-11-02, #1373).
- **Reopening.** ⌘Z and ⇧⌘T reopen a closed Peek, as they reopen closed tabs (Arc). Closing one, by Esc, ⌘W, its button or a click outside, is a step of the window's undo history, Close Link Preview in the Edit menu: ⌘Z opens it again over the tab it was on, at the page it was at, growing from its link, and shows that tab first if another shows; ⇧⌘Z closes it. ⇧⌘T reopens it while no tab has closed since, as it reopens what closed last, and then ⌘Z has nothing of it to reopen. A private window keeps none, as it keeps no history. The user's alone: agents get no Peek (ours).
- **Meeting links.** A call's link skips Peek and opens as a tab at the top of Today in the Space shown, the one used last, which it shows (Arc), as a call outlasts a look: Google Meet's, Zoom's and Microsoft Teams' meeting pages, and Webex's, Whereby's and Jitsi Meet's (ours). A Google Meet link from another app opens there too, by the Meet rule a profile starts with, as Arc seeds its Google Meet route (Link rules); the other services' links from other apps go by the rules, as Zoom's and Teams' usually open their own apps. With Peek turned off, a call's link goes where any link does.

### Little Arc

Arc's keys are recorded: ⌥⌘N, and ⌘O to open the page in the main window. The behavior below is Ardia's version (P§1), modeled on Arc and not measured from it. Ours is Little ilo, a window with a Space of its own that's for one of the user's Spaces, its *home*: the Space the window it came from showed, in that Space's profile. Its Space isn't saved or listed, and no other window shows it, as a private window's isn't (Profiles › Incognito).

- **Opening.** ⌥⌘N, File › New Little ilo Window and the command bar's New Little ilo Window open an empty one, whose command bar ⌘L opens, and ⌥⌘-click on a link opens the link in one (the owner's call, 2026-10-29), from a regular window, as a private window's links stay private. ⌥⌘-click on a Today tab's row in the sidebar opens that tab in one (Arc): ours moves the tab out of the Space, its page live with its history, as ⌘O moves it back, and the main window shows what it would once the tab closed. A pinned tab or a favorite keeps its place, and a split view's row, a folder's and an agent's Space's take ⌥-click's split instead (*interim*). A small window with one page and no sidebar, centred a little above the middle of the screen the pointer is on (Arc): ours, 0.4 of the room above it (*interim*), and on Linux where the window manager places it, as for any window. It opens on the current macOS desktop at its last size, never its last position. The first size is 900 × 640 (**Ardia**). Ours: the size the last one closed at, out of full screen, no bigger than the main screen. It grows in from a small rectangle under the pointer to its frame in about 0.12 s, 6 to 7 frames, its content laid out at full size, with no fade (an outside reverse-engineering of Arc 1.168, *interim*). Ours: from a 40 × 28 rectangle, easing out, under the pointer when a click opened it, in another app too, and at its middle when a key in the app did, as a surface a key opens comes where it always does. The window's drawing is scaled, not its frame, so its page isn't laid out again on each frame, and it waits at the rectangle until its UI has drawn on screen, so the start is seen. Under Reduce Motion it's at its frame at once, and on Linux and Windows it opens as the window manager opens any window (*interim*).
- **A full window.** Arc's works as any window does: extensions, and its videos going full screen in it, open on the current macOS desktop, and over a full-screen Arc window. Ours: it comes to the desktop shown each time it comes on screen, when it opens and when the same link, the Dock's Show All or Switch to Tab bring it back, never taking the user to the desktop it was on. Opened while a full-screen browser window has that desktop, it opens over it there, as an auxiliary window, the only way macOS lets a window join another's full-screen desktop, so that one can't go full screen itself (*interim*: what its page's full screen does there is for the Mac run to show). On Linux and Windows the window manager places it, on the workspace shown. Extensions are its profile's, as in any of its windows. Its pinned ones end the bar's title, as they end the URL field: buttons on the field's pitch, before Copy Link, the title giving up their room, an overflow button taking the last place when more are pinned than fit, a click running one with its popup hanging from its button and a right-click giving its menu (ours, #693). A page in it goes full screen there, as a video's button takes it, not to the main window, and its escape leaves full screen without closing the window.
- **A page's question.** With no URL field to hang from, it hangs under the bar at the card's left, and the page's card starts under it until it's answered, as nothing draws over the page (ours, #1914).
- **Bar.** A thin bar in the Space colour holds the window buttons, favicon, host and title, then Copy Link and a split button, "Open in <Space> ⌘O | ⌄" (an outside reverse-engineering of Arc 1.168, *interim*; Ardia's bar before it). A click on the bar, ⌘L or ⌘T opens Arc's command bar inside the window, 500 wide, its top a third of the way down, the URL filled in and selected. The button names the Space the page goes to, and its chevron opens a 240 pt menu hanging from the window's top right: Search Spaces…, then a 54 pt row for each Space, which typing filters and ↩ moves the tab to. ⌘O moves the live page, with history, form text, scroll and playing media, into the main window and closes Little Arc. Ours: the main window's header row, its buttons the header's with their tooltips and the favicon in the sidebar button's place, in its home's colour, theme and name, which follow it, and host and title each end in an ellipsis. ⌘L and ⌘T open the command bar inside the window, ⌘T as ⌘L since the window holds one page, while a click on the title opens the compact bar over it, as a surface a click opens sits on what was clicked. The split button ends the bar where its last button did: "Open in" and its home's name in 13 pt medium, then ⌘O's key as Settings › Shortcuts has it, dimmer, together no wider than 200 pt and ending in an ellipsis, with no tooltip as the words say it; a hairline; then the chevron, with no tooltip, as the profile picker has none. The chevron's menu is the compact command bar, 240 wide, its top 4 under the chevron and its right edge 8 in from the window's, as every overlay's is, 4 inside the chevron's, with Search Spaces… and a row for each of the user's Spaces in the sidebar's order, its home's too and no agent's, each with Move to Space's swatch, at the bar's 50 pt rows rather than 54 (*interim*). Typing filters them by a word's start, and ↩ moves the page live to that Space's Today in the main window, as ⌘O does; to a Space in another profile it loads the page again there, asked first as Move to Space asks. ⌘O, the split button's words and the command bar's Open in Main Window put the page at the top of its home's Today in the regular window showing it, else the one in front, switched to it, which shows the page and comes to the front; a home deleted or an agent's since, the main window's Space. ⌘O is Little ilo's alone: elsewhere it does nothing until Open File (*interim*). Its commands are a private window's, less those for other tabs, the sidebar and splits.
- **Closing.** ⌘W, the close button or Esc (when the page doesn't use it) closes the window and drops the page. The page stays in History, and ⇧⌘T reopens it in Little Arc with its back/forward list. Ours: Esc closes it once there's no load for it to stop and no page full screen for it to leave. Its page goes in the archive as a closed Today tab does, marked as Little ilo's, and in the closed tabs ⇧⌘T reopens, from any regular window or Little ilo window: a new Little ilo window for its home (the window's own Space if that's in the page's profile, else the first of the user's Spaces in it), with its history while the browser has run since it closed, which comes to the front. Restored from the Library it comes back as any archived tab does, at the top of the Today of the Space shown (ours). Closing the last regular window quits, closing it too, as it does a private one, and a quit archives nothing.
- **Links from other apps** open in a new Little Arc, and the same link again brings back the one it opened while that's open (Arc); a setting switches this to "a new tab". The page's own links stay in it. `target=_blank` and ⌘-click open as tabs of the main window, behind. Ours: a tab its page opens, by ⌘-click, `target=_blank` or ⌥-click, goes to the top of its home's Today, behind, and while no window shows it, the Little ilo window asks over its own page what that tab asks to open another app for, as a sign-in's pop-up hands back to its app; its own page's dialog comes first (ours, #1914). A pop-up its page asks for with `window.open()` and a window's features, as a sign-in's does, opens as a Little ilo window of its own over it instead (ours, #1919), as Chrome's opens as a window: its page at the size it asked, under a Little ilo bar, where it asked or centred on its opener 10 under its top (Chrome's cascade), kept on a screen. It's still a tab (decision 3), and once its page closes itself, its window goes and the Little ilo window that opened it has the keyboard back. Links from other apps open in one by default, and Settings › Links' Everything else switches that to The Space in Front, a new tab, or a Space (Link rules).
- **Where links land.** Ardia uses the profile of the window used last. Ours: the Space used last.
- **Profile.** Little Arc remembers the profile chosen for a domain and preselects it for new links from that domain, opening in the right Space (#693). Ours, as no capture exists (designed from ux-principles and the bar around it): while the user has more than one profile, the bar's buttons start with a profile picker, a header button like Copy Link's, the header's pitch before it, with the Profiles glyph Settings' Profiles section uses. It runs no command, so it has no tooltip. With one profile it isn't there and the title has its room (Quiet controls: a control with nothing to act on is noise). A click shows the menu the app's other menus are, hanging from the button: the profiles by name, the page's checked, as a Space's Profile items list them. Another opens the page's URL in a new Little ilo window in that profile, for the window's home if that's in it, else the first of the user's Spaces in it, and closes this one; the page loads fresh there, as a profile's cookies are its own (decision 5). Either choice is kept for the site, its host less www., and a link from another app to that site that opens in Little ilo opens in that profile, for the Space in front if that's in it, else its first Space. The app keeps the sites in its profile directory, as `Little ilo profiles`, by the profile's name, which follows a rename, and forgets a deleted profile's. Picking the other profile again is the way back. Only links from other apps follow it: a page's own links stay in the page's profile, and ⌥⌘N opens in the profile of the Space shown.
- **Linux.** The desktop entry has a New Little ilo Window action, which a launcher's menu on ilo's icon offers, as the Dock's item does on the Mac: it runs `ilo --new-little-window`, which opens one for the Space shown, launching the browser if it isn't running (ours, #693). Linux has nothing for Show or Hide All, as a launcher's actions don't change with what's open, nor a tray every desktop shows (GNOME has none), so ilo has no button there.
- **Archive.** An open Little Arc window closes into the archive after 6 hours by default, with the time in Settings › Features, and View Archive has a Little Arc filter (#693). Ours: the clock starts when the window stops being the active one, hidden or not, and counts on the wall clock, so one left behind closes as if by ⌘W; one whose page plays sound waits another interval, as a Today tab does. Settings › Features' Archive windows left unused after, under Little ilo, offers 1 hour, 6 hours (default), 12 hours and 24 hours, kept with the Spaces for every profile, as a Little ilo window belongs to no profile's list (ours, beyond Arc's default). Archived Tabs' Filter button shows while the archive holds a Little ilo tab or a task, and its Little ilo lists Little ilo's tabs alone (Library › Archived Tabs).
- **Dock.** Right-clicking the Dock icon offers Show or Hide All Little Arc Windows (Arc). Ours: the Dock menu has New Window and New Incognito Window, File's, in its titles and order, which run as ⌘N and ⇧⌘N do in the regular window in front, in place of the engine's, which opened Chrome's windows (Arc's Dock menu, #669); then New Little ilo Window, for the Space the regular window in front shows, as a link from another app gets; then, while one is open, Hide All Little ilo Windows, which takes them off screen without closing them, or once they're all hidden Show All Little ilo Windows, which brings them back with the one used last in front, out of the Dock if minimized. A hidden one comes back too when the same link brings it, and the Window menu lists it. On Windows the taskbar button's jump list has a Tasks section with New window and New incognito window, and New Little ilo Window, which runs the browser with `--new-little-window` and opens one as the Dock's item does; ilo writes it as its first window opens, under the windows' AppUserModelID, and Windows keeps it (ours, #693). Windows has no Show or Hide All there, as a jump list's tasks don't change with what's open. Linux's desktop file has New window and New incognito window as its actions, in sentence case. Each of those launches ilo again with Chrome's --new-window or --incognito, and the running browser opens the window as ⌘N or ⇧⌘N does in the regular window in front; launched with --incognito while it wasn't running, its private window opens over the first (#669).
- **Menu bar.** Arc offers an optional menu bar button that opens Little Arc. Ours: Settings › Features' Show in the menu bar, under Little ilo, off at first, puts a button with a window-and-plus symbol in the menu bar, tinted as the menu bar's own, and a click on it opens New Little ilo Window as the Dock's item does and brings the app forward. Off, it's gone. The choice is the app's, not a profile's, and kept across launches. It does nothing until it's clicked, so an idle browser stays asleep. On Windows the same row is Show in the notification area, "An icon there opens a new one.", off at first: ilo's icon, named by its tooltip New Little ilo Window, goes in the notification area, in its hidden icons' flyout until the user drags it out, and a click opens one, as the menu bar's button does. It waits for the click and for Explorer's TaskbarCreated, which puts it back after Explorer restarts, with no timer (ours, #693). Linux has no tray every desktop shows (GNOME has none), so Settings offers no switch there.
- **Agents.** None yet: an agent opens tabs in its own Space, and Little ilo's Space is the user's window's own (*interim*). The profile kept for a site is the user's, never read or set by an agent (#693).
- Measure everything visual in Arc ourselves: size, bar height, radius, colours, the open and close animations, and how ⌘O moves the page. The outside values above stand until then.

- **Turning it off** (ours, #1636). Settings › Features' Little ilo row has a switch, on by default, its own settings under it showing only while it's on, which the core keeps with the Spaces (`bc_little_windows`). Off takes every way in away: ⌥⌘N does nothing, and File's New Little ilo Window, the command bar's row, the Dock menu's items, Settings › Shortcuts' rows and the menu bar's button go, the button's own choice kept; ⌥⌘-click on a link opens it in a tab behind, as ⌘-click does, and on a Today tab's row it's a click; links from other apps, and rules to Little ilo, open in The Space in Front (Link rules › Everything else); ⇧⌘T reopens a Little ilo window's tab as a Today tab; and the core opens none for anyone. The windows open stay, with ⌘O. The switch's line says what's gone while it's off, and the switch is the way back. Agents have no path to it.
### Link rules

Arc's Air Traffic Control (Settings › Links): rules that send a link from another app to the Space it belongs in, so a work link opens signed in to work. Arc's isn't captured; what follows is ours (*interim*) until take 8 measures it, but for what a rule matches, measured in Arc 1.170 from outside, with links handed over by `open -g -a Arc` (#692).

- **A rule** is a condition and a Space, the Space in front or Little ilo, as Arc's route goes to a Space, Most Recent Space or Little Arc (#692). The condition is the URL containing some text or being equal to it, as Arc 1.170's were measured (#692): both ignore case, the scheme and a leading www., on the URL and the text alike. "Contains" looks in all of what's left, the query and the fragment too. "Is equal to" compares the host and the path alone, so the query, the fragment, a user and a trailing slash don't count, and a longer path doesn't meet it; a port does count, as one typed names another server (ours, unmeasured). The link coming from an app (the sender macOS reports for the open), or both, which both have to match, once the host reports the sender. A rule with no text yet matches nothing. Rules run top to bottom and the first match wins; a drag reorders them.
- **What they route.** What Settings › Links from other apps covers: a link clicked in another app, `open https://…`, an .html file from Finder, including one that launches the browser. And a link a plain click would open in a link preview, as Arc routes a Peek's sites (the owner, #692): a rule to another Space than the one shown opens it there as a tab instead, landing as below, while a rule to the Space shown or to Little ilo, a quick look as the preview is, and everything else, leave it in the preview, since Everything else is for links from other apps (ours). Not from a private window. Never a page's other links, a link the user asked to see in Peek (Peek › Which clicks open it), the command bar or an agent's tabs.
- **The Meet rule.** A profile starts with one rule, URL contains meet.google.com, to the Space in front, as Arc seeds a meet.google.com route to Most Recent Space once (`didSetDefaultGoogleMeetRoute`, measured in Arc 1.170 from outside, #692): a call opens as a tab where the user is, not in a Little ilo window, where everything else goes. A profile from before gets it after its own rules. It's a rule like any other, so the trash takes it, and the file then says it was added, so it doesn't come back, as Arc's flag's name says its route is set once. Only Meet's, as Arc's: the other meeting links from other apps go by the rules (Peek › Meeting links).
- **Landing.** A match opens where Links from other apps would put it, but in the rule's Space: a new tab at the top of its Today, in the regular window showing that Space, or else the front regular window switched to it, which comes to the front. The Space's profile comes with it (decision 5). A rule to Little ilo opens it in a new Little ilo window for the user's Space the regular window in front shows, in its profile, which comes to the front (Little Arc). A rule to the Space in front opens it as Everything else's The Space in Front does, and leaves a link preview's link in the preview, as that's the Space shown.
- **Everything else** opens in Little ilo, as Arc's opens in Little Arc (the owner, 2026-10-31), until the user picks another. With Little ilo off, everything else and a rule to it open in The Space in Front, the pop-ups don't offer it, a rule to it says "Little ilo is off" on its pop-up, and the choice stays for when it's on again (#1636). A box 20 under + holds a row, Everything else opens in, with a pop-up: The Space in Front, then the user's Spaces, then Little ilo, checked by default. The Space in Front is a new tab in the front window's Space, as Links from other apps has it.
- **Settings.** A Links tab after General, with Lucide's link (Arc's place: measure). One box holds the rules, a 40-tall row each: the condition's pop-up, 135 wide, URL contains or URL is equal to; the text's field, filling what's left, 24 tall in the controls' grey with its text 6 in and example.com as its placeholder; Lucide's arrow-right in grey; the Space's pop-up, 140 wide, listing The Space in Front, the user's Spaces, then Little ilo, as Arc's lists Most Recent Space, every Space and Little Arc (#692), a name too long for it ending in an ellipsis; and Lucide's trash at its end, a 24-square button, as a thing removed takes the trash (ux-principles); 8 apart. ⌘Z, Ctrl+Z off the Mac, puts the last rule the trash took back at its place, with its text, until Settings closes; a rule with nothing typed goes without a trace. There's no toast: the row leaving says what happened, as Clear's do (#1377). From [app pop-up, None first] goes before the arrow once the host reports the sender. + under the box, 11 below it, adds a rule at the end, URL contains, to The Space in Front, as Arc's New Route appends Contains, empty, Most Recent Space (#692), and puts the keyboard in its field. A rule's row drags by anywhere but its controls, the row under the pointer taking the controls' grey, and lets go onto another row to take its place. With no rules, once the trash has taken the Meet rule, the box says, in one grey line, "Rules send links from other apps to the Space they belong in." The text is kept as it's typed.
- **Limits.** The Space pop-up lists the user's Spaces only, never an agent's, and a rule never opens a private window. A rule whose Space is deleted is skipped and its row says No Space, so ⌘Z bringing the Space back brings the rule back too.
- **Agents.** No path and no new tool: rules are the user's, like the default browser, and Little ilo is a place of the user's UI. An agent's links open in its own Space, as they did, never by a rule or in Little ilo.
- **Import.** Arc's rules come in with its Spaces, read from `StorableLinkRouting.json` beside its sidebar, in Arc's order after the user's own: a rule to a Space goes to the Space its Arc Space became, one to Little Arc to Little ilo, and one to the most recent Space to the Space in Front, as the public readers of Arc's file read its destinations (vane #490). A rule the user has, to the same place, isn't added again. Arc's Everything else, the file's `defaultDestination`, comes too, unless it's Little Arc, ours' default, and only while the user's is still Little ilo, so a choice made here stays. A rule that matches the sending app stays in Arc, as no host reports the sender, as does one to a Space that doesn't come, and so does Arc's switch for opening links from other apps in Little Arc, which it keeps in its defaults, not the file. The summary counts the rules, Everything else among them, and the Spaces switch says they come.
- **Platforms.** The app keeps the rules in its profile directory, in `Link rules`, as it keeps `Shortcuts`, each rule's Space by its place among the user's Spaces, as the core's ids are new each launch and the Spaces come back in their order; the file is written again when a Space moves or goes, and a Space gone is written as none. Little ilo is written by its name and the Space in front as `front`, a line says the Meet rule was added, and Everything else's line is left out while it's the default, so a profile from before Little ilo opens links in it too. Where a platform doesn't report the sending app, the From pop-up isn't shown; no host reports it yet.
- Measure in Arc: the Links tab and its rows, the fallback's choices, and whether Arc offers a rule when a tab from another app is moved to another Space.

### Themes

Arc's theme, read by Ardia from Arc 1.167's shader and the code that feeds it (P§21, `profileTheme.ts`), and Arc 1.170's picker, measured from the owner's recording (Arc 1.170 rec at 2×, 15.5–31.2 s). The theme is per Space.

- **The picker** (Arc 1.170 rec). A card 357 × 509 at radius 20 with the icon picker's fill and edge, beside the sidebar, its arrow pointing left at the form's Choose a Theme row, the card centred on the arrow. It grows out of the arrow over ≈ 310 ms with a small overshoot, and a click outside shrinks it toward the arrow and fades it in ≈ 110 ms. From the top:
  - **Field.** A 340 pt square 8 inside the card, radius 12, white 0.03 with a 1 pt rim of white 0.2 and 1 pt dots of white 0.25 on a 4.3 pitch. It's an HSL disc about its middle: hue is the angle clockwise from the right, saturation 1, lightness the distance over half its width, so black in the middle and white at the edge. Empty, it says "Tap to pick a color for this space", 13 semibold white, and a click puts the first colour there.
  - **Handles.** The main colour is a 44 pt disc of its colour with a 3 pt white ring, the others 26. Pressed, a handle grows to 48 / 28 in ≈ 60 ms and back on release. Dragging the main one moves them all. With two colours the second is the first's opposite, hue + 180° at the same distance; with three they're + 150° and + 210°, and the second glides from + 180° over ≈ 200 ms.
  - **Appearance.** Three buttons over the field's top, 40.4 apart, 49 down: automatic, light and dark, as sparkles, sun and moon with stars (ours are Lucide's), 16 pt at white 0.65, the chosen one on a 31.5 pt plate at radius 6, white 0.08.
  - **− and +** under the field, 40 apart, 12 pt glyphs at white 0.35, brighter while pressed: one colour fewer or more, from one to three.
  - **Swatches.** Nine 24 pt discs on a 29.5 pitch, each with a 1.5 pt ring at 0.88 of its fill: cream, pink, purple, red, orange, yellow, green, blue and slate (sRGB F0EAE5, E59EBA, 9E749B, E1676F, EF8C63, F2D46C, 73E49B, 7EB6D5, 656786).
  - **Intensity**, a wave slider: a 224 × 16 track of white 0.1 with a sine of 7 periods over it, 4 pt, white 0.55 left of the knob and 0.25 right, its amplitude 15 × intensity. The knob is a white capsule 23 × 55.5, its middle travelling 201 pt. A theme starts at 0.81. Left, it washes the window's colours out.
  - **Grain**, a dial: 24 dots of 4 pt on a 32 pt circle, those swept bright, the rest dim, round a disc of radius 20 with a white 0.3 rim and a darker fill. The knob, a 7 × 15 white capsule on the dots, starts at the top with no grain and turns clockwise, and the noise shows over the window and in the disc.
  - With no colour yet, the slider and the dial are dimmer and have no knobs.
  - The window takes every change as it's made, each frame of a drag.
- **Rendering.**
  - Colours 1 and 2 mix in OKLab along a 45° diagonal, with colour 2 reached at 75 % of the way. A third colour fades in from the top to the bottom.
  - The layer's alpha is 0.2 + 0.55 × intensity. It sits over a white (light) or black (dark) underlay, over the blur behind the window.
  - The texture is multiplied in at noise × its strength: Arc's four textures have strengths 0.8, 0.15, 0.6 and 0.4. Inactive windows show the texture at a quarter strength, and the blur goes opaque.
  - In Arc 1.170 the automatic appearance turned the window light, one flat fill, for a light colour (230, 255, 35).
  - Our textures and their names are our own.
- Ours (*interim*, `app/src/theme_picker.rs`, `app/src/shell/themes.rs`), where the recording doesn't show Arc's:
  - The appearance is drawn: light and dark force the window's look, and automatic follows the system's appearance (Window material), not the colour's lightness, as Arc 1.170's turned the window light for a light colour. A theme of two looks, which only Arc's import makes, shows its dark look in a dark window and its own in a light one; the picker edits the look shown and makes one look of it.
  - One colour keeps the Space gradient, the colour to HSL lightness + 0.25 down the window, at its alpha at 0.81, so a theme of one colour at the start looks as the colour did before themes. Intensity scales that alpha as 0.2 + 0.55 × intensity does Arc's. Two or three colours lie over the window as Arc's rule has them, at the same alpha.
  - Grain is a tile of one of our four textures at one device pixel a pixel, over the window and in the dial's disc: Noise, every pixel its own, at 0.075 of the dial's turn; Dust, scattered specks; Weave, diagonal threads; and Fleck, short fibres over a fainter noise. Each is as white as it is black. Arc's strengths go with its own images, which don't come in, so each of ours is set to move the window's colours as much as Noise does at the same dial (`app/design/tokens.toml`). A theme from the picker has Noise; one from Arc keeps its texture through the picker's edits, as the recorded picker has no way to choose one. An inactive window shows it at a quarter, as Arc's does.
  - A third colour from Arc is kept exactly, in the window and its swatches, until the picker moves a colour, changes the count or picks a swatch, when the field's colour at its handle takes its place. Its handle shows it at its own hue and lightness in the field, where the import places Arc's other colours.
  - A swatch gives the main colour exactly the swatch's, its handle at the swatch's hue and lightness in the field, and the other colours the swatch's saturation too, keeping the count, intensity, grain and appearance; a handle dragged after it takes the field's colour, at saturation 1. Arc's swatch click isn't in the recording. Arc's < and >, which page the swatches, aren't drawn.
  - − is dimmed at one colour and + at three. Another colour than the main one drags alone to a place of its own, and keeps it round the main one as that moves: as far round the field's middle, and as much further out or in. − and + put the colours back at the set angles, the second gliding there over the 0.2 s Arc's glide takes. Handles stay in the field. A click on the field with a colour does nothing. An appearance chosen before the first colour is kept for it. Escape closes the picker.
  - The keyboard (#1383): Tab and ⇧Tab go round the appearance buttons, − and +, the swatches and the wave, top to bottom, each landing on the appearance chosen, on whichever of − and + answers, or on the first swatch. ← and → go along the buttons or swatches, stopping at the ends, or move the wave's knob by 0.05, onto those steps. Space and ↩ press what's ringed, as a click does, one step for ⌘Z. Without a colour, − and + and the wave are passed over, as their clicks do nothing. The ring is Focus Sidebar's, 2 pt in the ink at 0.45, inside a button's plate and 1 pt round a swatch or the knob. It shows from the first Tab, and a click takes it away. The handles and the dial stay the mouse's: a swatch places a colour as a click on the field does, and the grain is a finishing touch.
  - Between Spaces of more than one colour the window cross-fades the two layers as the pages slide. What covers the window takes its colour at the sidebar's middle.
  - The card keeps 8 from the window's top and bottom, its arrow moving along its side.
- **Appearance.** View › Appearance › Automatic, Light, Dark (Arc, Dia). What Arc's does isn't recorded. Ours:
  - It's the app's, for every window, and stands in for the system's appearance: Light or Dark draws a Space with no theme, or an automatic one, that way, and Automatic follows the system again. A theme that forces light or dark keeps it, as the Space's own choice is the nearer one, and a private window stays dark.
  - The menu, its native items, alerts and pages follow it as they follow the system's, so a page's `prefers-color-scheme` does too.
  - Pages never read a Space's theme (owner, 2026-10-29, #684): they get only light or dark, View › Appearance's or the system's, as every Chromium page does, and the system's own colours, never the Space's, which would tell sites apart from the user's Spaces. Arc's Settings › Advanced "Allow websites to get your theme data" has no counterpart.
  - The choice is checked in the menu and kept for the next launch, beside the keys. The command bar offers Automatic, Light and Dark Appearance, leaving out the one chosen, and Settings › Shortcuts can give each a key. No default key, as Arc's menu shows none.
  - The user's preference, so agents have no call to change it, as with the favorites' scope.
  - Haptics (decided by the owner, 2026-10-29): the trackpad gives one detent as a drag reaches a stop, a handle the field's rim, the intensity knob either end or the dial the top, and none while it's held there or as it moves freely. It follows Settings' haptics switch, as the Space swipe's detent does, and Linux and Windows have none.
- Measure Arc's swatch clicks, its < and >, −, an appearance button, a secondary handle dragged alone, the picker from the Space's menu, and the keyboard in the picker.

### Mini Player

Arc calls its Picture in Picture the Mini Player. Its values are Dia's (Dia 1.50.1 rec, D›Picture in Picture) until Arc's are measured.

- **Turning it off.** Arc's Site Controls have a Picture in Picture switch for the site, and its Advanced settings turn it off everywhere. Ours is Chrome's automatic picture-in-picture: a page that asks for it with its media session floats as its tab hides: a video playing with sound on a site the user plays media on often, or a call. Site Controls list the site's Automatic picture-in-picture once the page has asked, with Allow, Block and Ask, Ask floating with Chrome's question on the window. Settings › Features' Mini Player switch blocks it by default in every profile, so a site allowed in its Site Controls still floats; turned on, the default is Chrome's again. Arc's Mini Player menu, which turns it off for the site for a day or for good, waits for the Mini Player to be ours.
- **Moving, sizing and going back** (Arc's; how far is ours, *interim*). A video's Mini Player moves with a scroll over it, as far as the content would go, and with a drag anywhere off its controls; ⌘ with a scroll (Ctrl off the Mac) or a pinch sizes it about its middle, 1.1 × a wheel notch, keeping its shape, within Chrome's least and greatest. Its corners and edges resize it as Chrome's do, and it stays on the display it's mostly on. A double-click off its controls goes back to its tab, as its back-to-tab button does: the window holding the tab shows it, in its Space. AppKit hands Chromium's windows no pinch yet, so on the Mac it's ⌘-scroll. A call's Mini Player is a page of its own, which scrolls as a page does and moves by its top, as Chrome's.
- **Where it shows, and going back as its window comes back** (Arc 1.168, an outside reverse-engineering, #1407; the size is Chrome's). A video's first Mini Player shows 10 pt from the top right of its screen's work area, the screen Chrome picks, its tab's window's. Arc's 782 × 440 is one sample with no rule, so the size stays Chrome's: a fifth of the work area each way, fitted to the video. After that each shows where the last was left, at that one's corner nearest its screen's, so a video of another shape sits as the last did, kept on the screen, across launches too, as Arc's, on the nearest screen if that one's gone. Once shown, Chrome's re-layouts keep it on its screen, not at Chrome's margin. When the user comes back from another app or window to the window showing its tab, the Mini Player closes and the video plays on in the page, as Arc's does, one the page floated itself too. The command bar, a menu or the Mini Player taking the keyboard isn't leaving; going to another app or window with one of them open is. A call's floating page stays (#700). A tab switch inside the window keeps Chrome's rule: an automatic one lands and one the page floated stays.
- **Its keys** (Chrome's, *interim*). With the Mini Player key, Space plays or pauses and ← and → seek 10 s, and the keyboard's media keys play or pause it, as Chrome's do; a page's media session gives it previous and next track. Arc's back and forward 15 s wait for buttons of ours. The playback speed is the page's, which the Mini Player keeps.

- At rest: the video only, with 6 pt circular corners, a 1 pt rim of white 0.17 inside the edge and a soft shadow. It appears ≈ 125 ms after the tab switch with no animation.
- On hover: a black 0.35 scrim fades in over 200 ms, ease-in-out. Back-to-tab (28 × 28) and close (29 × 28) buttons sit 12 from the top and their sides, radius ≈ 6, white 0.09 (≈ 0.13 under the pointer). The origin shows in 13 pt, centred, baseline 30. Pause is two 7 × 42 capsules 10 apart. A 5 pt progress bar runs from 12 to width − 12, 9 above the bottom; the played part is white, the rest white ≈ 0.27.
- Close fades it out in ≈ 100 ms and the video keeps playing in its tab. Back to tab fades it out in ≈ 70 ms.
- Throw (Dia 1.53.0 bin): a release faster than 350 pt/s (or a last swipe delta over 6 pt) throws it to the screen corner within 45°. It lands 10 pt in, on a spring of 0.9 s / ζ 0.9 that starts at the throw velocity. A slow release stays where it is let go.

### Agents UI

See Spaces › Agent Space and Agent task, whose card an outside agent's work has too (Agent task › Outside agents). The confirmation UI and the activity log ship in the same change as the MCP server (decision 8).

- **Scripts** (ours, #704; Arc has an AppleScript dictionary). On the Mac, AppleScript reads the windows, front to back, never a private one; the user's Spaces, as the switcher lists them, never an agent's; and a Space's tabs, pinned then Today, with their titles and URLs. A window's tabs are the Space it shows. `focus` shows a Space and `select` a tab, as a click does; `make new window` opens one as ⌘N does, and `make new tab` with a URL opens it in a window's Space or a Space of a window as ⌘T would, or at the application as a link from another app does, by Settings › Links' rules. Nothing reads a page, its cookies or its passwords, nothing runs JavaScript, a tab opens only an http or https page, and no property can be set. The dictionary is ilo's (`app/platform/mac/ilo.sdef`): the app never answers Chromium's, which the engine declares. macOS asks the sending app once, but a terminal it said yes for lets every process under it in, agents too, so Settings › Agents' "Let scripts control the browser", off at first, refuses every script, reads too, with "Scripts are off in ilo's Settings › Agents."; it's the way back out. Each Apple Event is one entry under "What scripts did", refused or not: what it did and on what, the app that sent it, or a tool with the app it runs in ("osascript in Terminal"), and when. Linux and Windows have no Apple Events and show neither.

- **What agents may do** (ours). `bc_agent_may_act` lets an agent do only what a user does in a page, the reference agent's and the MCP server's tab tools: back, click, close_tab, forward, hand_off, navigate, press_key, read_page, screenshot, scroll, select_option, snapshot and type. It refuses the rest whatever the tab, saying why in the activity-log entry: ad_blocking, devtools and passwords each with their own reason, any other as an action agents don't have. A refused call starts no session. Until the activity log has a window, these rows tick from the refusals the agents run asserts in its log.
- **A request's row** (ours, #2326, the owner's call). In Settings › Agents' activity, each request an agent sent, or that was refused, opens: a click, Space or Return on its row. "To" gives its whole address; "Sent", or "Not sent" for one refused, gives its body a line a field, keys at the detail's grey and values at the ink. A form's fields are decoded, JSON's nest by indent, a list of plain values joins on one line, and other text keeps its lines. A secret's value, by the rule that hides them from an agent's answers, is "(hidden)" in grey, and the log never keeps it. Eight lines show at most, each cut with an ellipsis at the row's width; past eight, the eighth is "Show all N lines", which opens the rest in place, a long value then wrapping whole. A row's chevron sits at its end after its time, and a row with nothing to open keeps its place empty, so every time ends on one edge. Library › Agents' steps open the same way. It's kept with the activity, at most 200 lines a request and 2000 characters a value, in the profile only and never synced.

## Picked by the owner, not on the roadmap

Boosts are Arc's. Ad blocking, environments and the Dock dot go beyond Arc and Dia; the owner picked them (decision 6). Add them to a phase before building them, as ad blocking is, in Phase 3, and Boosts are, in Phase 4; the Dock dot is built.

### Boosts

Arc kept Boosts in its account and shared them in a gallery. Ours are local files with no account (principle 2). Phase 4 (#695) builds CSS and Zap; a Boost's script waits for a second slice of its own, as it runs code in the user's pages and needs its own review.

- **What a Boost is** (Arc). CSS the user writes for one site, plus Zap: click an element to hide it. A site is the page's host without a leading "www.", for http(s) pages only, so a Boost for example.com shows on www.example.com and not on news.example.com (ours: Arc's is the domain, which a measure can replace).
- **Storage** (Flecha). One plain file per site, `Boosts/<site>.css` in the profile's folder, so the user can keep the folder in git. A Boost belongs to its profile, as its logins do, and every Space of the profile shows it. Which sites have theirs off is a list kept beside the folder, `Boosts off`, so the folder holds nothing but the user's CSS.
- **Applying** (Flecha). The CSS shows on the site's pages as they load and at once on every save of its file, by the user's editor or anything else, with no reload: the app watches the folder. The core adopts it as a constructed style sheet in Chrome's own isolated world, which the page's scripts can't see; a page that replaces `document.adoptedStyleSheets` drops it until its next load.
- **Making and editing.** New Boost in the command bar, the sidebar's + menu, a Today tab's menu (Arc's Customize Page…), Site Controls' Boost tile (the second, after Share) and New Boost… in its "…" menu open the card for the site of the page shown, under the URL field where a site's question hangs, a tab's menu showing its tab first; with a Boost each is Edit Boost. The menus offer neither on a page that can't have a Boost: a file, a private window or an agent's Space. The card gives the site, a line saying what its Boost does ("Hides 3 things and adds 12 lines of your CSS."), Edit CSS… and Zap, and under them, on a line of its own so Zap's row stays put as Zap starts, Customize…, which opens the editor in the card's place (Customizing). Edit CSS… opens the site's file in the user's own editor, the Mac's default text editor, Notepad, or what the Linux desktop opens it with, starting it with a comment that names the site. Zap turns the card to "Click what to hide": the page draws an outline round what's under the pointer, and each click hides it at once and adds its selector to the file, as `<selector> { display: none !important; } /* Zap */`. Undo takes the last back, and Done or escape, in the page or the UI, ends Zap; escape again closes the card. A new page ends Zap. The card and Zap's outline are ours, *interim* until Arc's are measured.
- **Customizing** (Arc's structure; the values and look ours). The editor without code is a card beside the sidebar in the theme picker's form, 357 wide with its arrow at the URL field, a dark sheet over either look. Its header is the site's icon, its page's favicon or a globe without one, then 8 after it the Boost's name with a menu (Rename…, Reset All Edits, Delete Boost…), Shuffle and the Boost's switch. Under it the theme picker's field holds two dots, the big one the page's ground and the small one its links, at rest as rings until placed; a press places the ground and drags it, and the hint in the field ("Drag a dot…") goes after the Boost's first drag and doesn't come back. Then Invert lightness, which turns the page's lightness round and its pictures back; a tile for each face the computer has (the site's own, Sans, Serif, Rounded on the Mac, Mono), none that falls back to another tile's; the page's size from 90 to 150 % in tens, as CSS; and the text's case. Advanced, a chip at the end of Invert lightness's line, opens three sliders under that line, the card growing down by them and all under them moving with it: the page's contrast and brightness from 50 to 150 % and its saturation from 0 to 200 %, in fives, each filled from 100 % in the middle, its value at the right as the size's is, and applied as a CSS filter on the page's root after Invert lightness's. A press on a track sets it there and drags it; the arrow keys move the slider last pressed a step. Reset, left of Advanced while it's open and dimmed while all three are at 100 %, puts them back, as Reset All Edits does too. Advanced stays open or closed for each Boost as the user left it, closed for a new one, and opening it isn't a change ⌘Z takes back. Shuffle picks two dots, a face and a case. Edit CSS… and Zap are in the footer. Each change shows at once on every page of the site, in each window, and is kept in one block of the site's file, between the editor's start and end comments, rewritten whole; the user's own lines and Zap's are never touched. ⌘Z takes each change back while the editor has the keyboard. Escape or a click outside closes it, as the theme picker does, with no ✕ or Done. Agents get no path to it: a Boost's CSS can carry a page's values out, which agents never get.
- **Seeing and turning off.** Site Controls has a switch for the site's Boost, after Developer Mode's and Safari's engine's, with Lucide's paintbrush and "Boost", tinted while on (Extensions › Site Controls). "…" adds View Boosts…, as the command bar and Window › View Boosts… do, which opens Library › Boosts: the profile's Boosts by site, each with what it does, dimmed while off, and a globe in place of a favicon, *interim*. A row's menu has Edit CSS…, Turn Off or Turn On, Show in Finder (the folder on Linux) and Delete…, which asks first, as the file goes. Settings › Features has a Boosts switch that turns them all off and takes away every way in, the commands, the tile, the rows and the Library's section, keeping the files for when it's on again.
- **Private windows and agents.** A private window shows its profile's Boosts but makes none, as it keeps nothing. An agent's Space offers none. A Boost's script will run inside the user's logged-in pages, where it can read what principle 3 keeps from agents, so agents get no path to read, write or turn on a Boost (decision 8): the MCP server has no Boost tool. Any local program can write to the folder, a coding agent included, so a script that changed outside the browser will stay off until the user turns it on again in the browser.

### Ad blocking

Helium and Flecha block ads and trackers by default; Arc and Dia don't. Ours does from a new install's first page, with nothing to install (Phase 3).

- **Default.** On in every profile, new and existing, and in private windows, on every site. No site is trusted to show its ads. Arc's uBlock Origin trusted YouTube; ours blocks there as anywhere, and Site Controls turn it off for YouTube as for any site (owner, 2026-10-29).
- **Engine.** Upstream uBlock Origin (gorhill's), pinned by version and sha256 in `app/ublock.ini`, with our key added to its manifest for a stable id, and its housekeeping timers made to run once after the work that needs them instead of re-arming, so they don't wake an idle browser (`app/fetch-ublock.sh`). Not Helium's fork: upstream's releases are the ones its users and list authors test. It's a Manifest V2 extension; ungoogled-chromium's patch keeps Manifest V2 on 155 and still on 156.0.8078.47-1. `app/build.sh` puts it in the framework's resources, and the core loads it into each profile at every launch, as `--load-extension` does: it updates with the browser, never from a store, and nothing about it is saved but its own storage. It's no extension to the user: the Extensions menu, the extension list, Site Controls' extensions and the toolbar never show it, so its popup, its dashboard and its action's keys never open, and Settings › Ad Blocking is all of its settings (#2107).
- **Settings for it.** Settings › Ad Blocking is the browser's own model, in the core behind one interface (`bc_ad_block_lists`, `core/ad_block.h`), which uBlock Origin applies today and a native engine can take over (#2069) without Settings changing. The core gives uBlock Origin its settings as managed storage, a policy under the platform's, so an administrator's policy still wins: its lists from the source below, checked weekly, and under `ilo` the lists to turn on and off and My filters. uBlock Origin reads managed storage only as it starts, so a module of ours in its background page (`app/ublock-settings.js`) applies `ilo` then and on each change of the policy, an event, through the calls its own dashboard makes. Beyond that and its housekeeping timers (Engine), uBlock Origin is as upstream ships it. The page, after the switch and the sites it's off on:
  - **My filters**: the user's own, one a row with Remove, then a field and Add, which ↩ presses too. A filter list's syntax, with element hiding. Kept with the Spaces for every profile; none until the user adds one, so uBlock Origin's own stay as they were.
  - **Filter lists**: the lists uBlock Origin ships (its `assets.json`), by its groups, headed Built-in, Ads, Privacy, Malware, Multipurpose, Annoyances and Regional, each by title with a switch. On as uBlock Origin has them by default: Built-in, Ads, Privacy, Malware and Multipurpose's defaults; the annoyance and regional lists off, whatever the locale (ours: a list for the user's language is a click away, and a stale regional list costs every page). A change applies at once in every profile; a list not kept yet is fetched then.
  - **An administrator's policy** for uBlock Origin that sets `toOverwrite.filterLists` chooses the lists, which then show On or Off with no switch; one that sets `toOverwrite.filters` sets My filters, shown with no Remove or field, "Set by your organization".
  - **Hide an element…** isn't there: uBlock Origin's picker is its own UI over the page, which this keeps out. A filter in My filters does it.
- **Turning it off.** Settings › Ad Blocking's Block ads and trackers turns it off in every profile; the browser keeps it with the Spaces. Site Controls' shield, the action row's last tile, says by its tint and glyph whether it blocks on this site, its tooltip with how many requests it blocked on the page as uBlock Origin counts them ("Ads and trackers blocked, 12 on this page"), and turns it the other way on a click, reloading the page so what it held back loads. It turns it off for the registrable domain (the host, for an IP address or localhost), kept in the profile, and in the profile's private windows, where the shield isn't shown. The shield isn't there for a page that isn't http(s), or with it off in Settings. Settings › Ad Blocking lists every site it's off on, every loaded profile's, in the order they were turned off, each a row with the site, its profile's name in grey when there's more than one profile, and a Turn On button, which lets it block there again without reloading a page open there; Settings' section goes when there are none (ours).
- **Filter lists.** uBlock Origin ships its lists, so the first launch blocks without the network. It checks them 37 s after a launch if they're out of date, then once a week while the browser runs, never more often (principle 1). Their source is a default service URL, `kFilterListsSource` in `core/ad_block.cc`, a forks' own `assets.json`; empty, it's the one uBlock Origin ships, naming its upstream hosts (principle 2).
- **First launch.** uBlock Origin lets requests through until it has read its lists, about 3 s on a new profile's first launch. Its own fix, holding requests until then, would hold the launch's first page too, so it stays off until the cost is measured against the launch gate.
- **Cost.** Page-load and memory numbers, with and without it, against the performance gate.
- **Agents.** They get no call that changes it, everywhere or for a site: it keeps trackers off the user's own pages (decision 8). The core refuses an agent's `ad_blocking` action, with why, in the activity log (Agents UI). An agent's pages are blocked as the user's are.

### Environments

From Flecha: one project's origins, such as local, staging and production.

- **Setup.** The user adds, edits and removes a project's origins in Settings.
- **Switching** (Flecha). ⌥⌘E (*interim*; check for clashes) opens the same page on the project's next origin. Only the origin changes; the path, query and hash stay. The command bar lists the page's other origins.
- **Badge** (Flecha). The URL field names the environment of the page's origin. Production is red, so it's never mistaken for a safe place to click around.
- **Agents.** On a production origin, an agent asks first, as for pay and send (decision 8). Owner to confirm.

### Dock dot

The green dot on ilo's Dock icon takes the color of the key window's Space, so the Dock says which Space you're in. A Space with no color, or a private window, shows the green.

- **Native look.** The dot is recolored on macOS's own drawing of the icon, so it keeps the style's glass. In the Tinted and Clear icon styles, the icon stays exactly as macOS draws it: a colored dot would be the one icon out of style in the Dock.
- **Never in the switch.** One swap takes the main thread 10–45 ms, more than the 16 ms a whole Space switch may take. The dot changes 400 ms after the last switch, once the slide has settled, so a run of quick switches is one swap.
- **Launch.** The icon takes the color it quit with while the Dock still bounces.
